Not really… or, I mean, I keep trying to think of something so I don’t sound dramatic. But the things that used to be “mine” don’t feel like anything now. But sometimes, late at night when I’m in the studio—well, it’s barely a studio, it’s just the spare room with my old paints and all the school supplies piled everywhere—I’ll catch myself thinking that it would be kind of a relief if I just didn’t wake up.\n\nI haven’t made a plan or anything like that. But the thoughts scare me. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I just don’t feel like myself anymore. I used to always have things I cared about—painting, hiking, choir, Sunday family get-togethers. I mean, my sister used to tease me because I’d be the one organizing who brought dessert, and now even if I have a free hour, I just sort of sit there. I don’t pick up the brushes, I don’t call anyone, I don’t even really care about going. That’s what finally made me think I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy the things you used to, or has the pleasure gone away?\n\nPatient: The pleasure’s mostly gone, yeah. I mean, I used to really make time for things—painting especially, and hiking, choir, the Sunday family stuff. My sister used to joke I treated those dinners like some kind of military operation with desserts and who was bringing what… and now, even if I’ve got a free hour, I just sit there. I don’t pick up the brushes or text anyone. It’s like I know those things used to matter to me, but I can’t really feel why.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think… about eight months now? It kind of started building when I took over this STEM-Arts curriculum initiative at the school. At first I told myself I was just tired or stretched thin—because, you know, teachers always have some new project with a catchy name—but it didn’t really let up. It just kept getting worse from there.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Not really… or, I mean, I keep trying to think of something so I don’t sound dramatic. But the things that used to be “mine” don’t feel like anything now. Painting, hiking, choir, even Sunday family dinners—I used to make time for all of it. I’d be the one fussing over who was bringing dessert, which is silly, but it mattered to me.\n\nNow if I have a free hour, I just sort of sit there. I’ll look at my paints or think, “Maybe I should call someone,” and then I just… don’t. I can’t seem to care about it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about death, or wishing you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I haven’t really wanted to say that out loud, because it sounds so awful. But sometimes, late at night when I’m in the studio—well, it’s barely a studio, it’s just the spare room with my old paints and all the school supplies piled everywhere—I’ll catch myself thinking that it would be kind of a relief if I just didn’t wake up.\n\nI haven’t made a plan or anything like that. But the thoughts scare me. They scared me enough that I finally told my husband I couldn’t keep trying to handle it by myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge most of the time?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly. Maybe on edge sometimes, but it’s hard for me to tell what’s what anymore. I keep thinking of “anxious” like before a parent-teacher conference or when the copier jams five minutes before class—sorry, that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nMost of the time I just feel… not right. But I’m not sure I’ve noticed it as anxiety specifically.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t really been keeping track of it. My clothes maybe fit differently sometimes, but then I think, well, some of them are old anyway, and I’ve had the same work pants forever. I don’t weigh myself much… the scale is in the bathroom closet behind all the towels, and I keep meaning to clean that out.\n\nSo… I don’t know if it’s been a significant amount. I haven’t noticed something obvious like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving more slowly than you used to or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I don’t think I’ve noticed that exactly. Some mornings I move around like I’m underwater, but then I’ll also get distracted and start rearranging papers or sharpening pencils for no real reason—though that might just be because our house is always kind of cluttered.\n\nTrouble sitting still… maybe sometimes? But not in a way I’ve really thought about. I guess I’m having a hard time knowing what counts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving slower than you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I have moments where I feel like I’m taking longer to get going, like putting my shoes on or getting out the door, but then I think maybe everyone feels like that some days.\n\nAt school I’ll notice the kids are already lined up and I’m still gathering the attendance sheets, and then I start thinking about whether I left my mug in the staff room, so… I don’t know if that’s actually moving slower or just me being scattered. Yeah… I think so. It’s less like I can’t physically start, and more like I just… can’t make myself care enough to begin.

I used to have things I’d look forward to—painting, going on hikes, choir practice, Sunday family get-togethers. I used to squeeze those in even when school was busy, you know, like I’d pack my bag the night before or leave my paints out on the table. Now if I have a free hour, I’ll just sit there. I’ll think, “I could paint,” or “I should call about choir,” and then I just don’t.

That’s kind of what finally made me come in, honestly. I don’t feel like myself anymore.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I just don’t feel like myself anymore. I used to always have things I cared about—painting, hiking, choir, Sunday family get-togethers. I mean, my sister used to tease me because I’d be the one organizing who brought dessert, and now even if I have a free hour, I just sort of sit there. I don’t pick up the brushes, I don’t call anyone, I don’t even really care about going. That’s what finally made me think I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy the things you used to, or has the pleasure gone away?\n\nPatient: The pleasure’s mostly gone, yeah. I mean, I used to really make time for things—painting especially, and hiking, choir, the Sunday family stuff. My sister used to joke I treated those dinners like some kind of military operation with desserts and who was bringing what… and now, even if I’ve got a free hour, I just sit there. I don’t pick up the brushes or text anyone. It’s like I know those things used to matter to me, but I can’t really feel why.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think… about eight months now? It kind of started building when I took over this STEM-Arts curriculum initiative at the school. At first I told myself I was just tired or stretched thin—because, you know, teachers always have some new project with a catchy name—but it didn’t really let up. It just kept getting worse from there.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Not really… or, I mean, I keep trying to think of something so I don’t sound dramatic. I’d be the one fussing over who was bringing dessert, which is silly, but it mattered to me.\n\nNow if I have a free hour, I just sort of sit there. I can’t seem to care about it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about death, or wishing you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I haven’t really wanted to say that out loud, because it sounds so awful. But sometimes, late at night when I’m in the studio—well, it’s barely a studio, it’s just the spare room with my old paints and all the school supplies piled everywhere—I’ll catch myself thinking that it would be kind of a relief if I just didn’t wake up.\n\nI haven’t made a plan or anything like that. But the thoughts scare me. They scared me enough that I finally told my husband I couldn’t keep trying to handle it by myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge most of the time?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I just don’t feel like myself anymore. I used to always have things I cared about—painting, hiking, choir, Sunday family get-togethers. I mean, my sister used to tease me because I’d be the one organizing who brought dessert, and now even if I have a free hour, I just sort of sit there. I don’t pick up the brushes, I don’t call anyone, I don’t even really care about going. That’s what finally made me think I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy the things you used to, or has the pleasure gone away?\n\nPatient: The pleasure’s mostly gone, yeah. I mean, I used to really make time for things—painting especially, and hiking, choir, the Sunday family stuff. My sister used to joke I treated those dinners like some kind of military operation with desserts and who was bringing what… and now, even if I’ve got a free hour, I just sit there. I don’t pick up the brushes or text anyone. It’s like I know those things used to matter to me, but I can’t really feel why.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think… about eight months now? It kind of started building when I took over this STEM-Arts curriculum initiative at the school. At first I told myself I was just tired or stretched thin—because, you know, teachers always have some new project with a catchy name—but it didn’t really let up. It just kept getting worse from there.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Not really… or, I mean, I keep trying to think of something so I don’t sound dramatic. But the things that used to be “mine” don’t feel like anything now. Painting, hiking, choir, even Sunday family dinners—I used to make time for all of it. I’d be the one fussing over who was bringing dessert, which is silly, but it mattered to me.\n\nNow if I have a free hour, I just sort of sit there. I’ll look at my paints or think, “Maybe I should call someone,” and then I just… don’t. I can’t seem to care about it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about death, or wishing you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I haven’t really wanted to say that out loud, because it sounds so awful. But sometimes, late at night when I’m in the studio—well, it’s barely a studio, it’s just the spare room with my old paints and all the school supplies piled everywhere—I’ll catch myself thinking that it would be kind of a relief if I just didn’t wake up.\n\nI haven’t made a plan or anything like that. But the thoughts scare me. They scared me enough that I finally told my husband I couldn’t keep trying to handle it by myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge most of the time?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly. Maybe on edge sometimes, but it’s hard for me to tell what’s what anymore. I keep thinking of “anxious” like before a parent-teacher conference or when the copier jams five minutes before class—sorry, that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nMost of the time I just feel… not right. But I’m not sure I’ve noticed it as anxiety specifically.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t really been keeping track of it. My clothes maybe fit differently sometimes, but then I think, well, some of them are old anyway, and I’ve had the same work pants forever. I don’t weigh myself much… the scale is in the bathroom closet behind all the towels, and I keep meaning to clean that out.\n\nSo… I don’t know if it’s been a significant amount. I haven’t noticed something obvious like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving more slowly than you used to or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I don’t think I’ve noticed that exactly. Some mornings I move around like I’m underwater, but then I’ll also get distracted and start rearranging papers or sharpening pencils for no real reason—though that might just be because our house is always kind of cluttered.\n\nTrouble sitting still… maybe sometimes? But not in a way I’ve really thought about. I guess I’m having a hard time knowing what counts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving slower than you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I have moments where I feel like I’m taking longer to get going, like putting my shoes on or getting out the door, but then I think maybe everyone feels like that some days.\n\nAt school I’ll notice the kids are already lined up and I’m still gathering the attendance sheets, and then I start thinking about whether I left my mug in the staff room, so… I don’t know if that’s actually moving slower or just me being scattered. I haven’t really noticed it in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like it's harder to initiate activities or get started with things than it used to be?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s less like I can’t physically start, and more like I just… can’t make myself care enough to begin.\n\nI used to have things I’d look forward to—painting, going on hikes, choir practice, Sunday family get-togethers. I used to squeeze those in even when school was busy, you know, like I’d pack my bag the night before or leave my paints out on the table. Now if I have a free hour, I’ll just sit there. I’ll think, “I could paint,” or “I should call about choir,” and then I just don’t.\n\nThat’s kind of what finally made me come in, honestly. I don’t feel like myself anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really been tracking it. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different, I don’t think… though some days I avoid anything with a waistband anyway, so that’s maybe not the best measure.\n\nWe do have a scale somewhere, but it’s in the bathroom closet behind all these old towels and cleaning stuff—I keep meaning to clear that out. So… no, I haven’t noticed any big weight change. At least not that I could say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or restless when you're sitting or standing still?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way I’ve really noticed. I mean, I’ll shift around in a chair if it’s uncomfortable—those little plastic classroom chairs are awful, honestly, I don’t know how the kids sit in them all day—and sometimes I tap a pen if I’m holding one.\n\nBut restless like I can’t sit still? I don’t know. I wouldn’t say that’s been a big thing. I’m probably more just… sitting there and losing track of time than fidgeting.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do. Probably more than I should.

It’s mostly with this curriculum project at school. If something goes wrong—even something small, like a lesson not landing right or materials not being ready—I start thinking I’m letting the students down. And then it turns into this whole… spiral, I guess. Like my parents worked so hard so I could go to college and have opportunities, and here I am messing up something I’m supposed to be good at.

I know, logically, curriculum stuff always has hiccups. Teachers adjust things all the time. But I still end up feeling like it’s my fault, like I’m wasting what people did for me.

I don’t pick up the brushes or text anyone. It’s like I know those things used to matter to me, but I can’t really feel why.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think… about eight months now? It kind of started building when I took over this STEM-Arts curriculum initiative at the school. At first I told myself I was just tired or stretched thin—because, you know, teachers always have some new project with a catchy name—but it didn’t really let up. It just kept getting worse from there.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Not really… or, I mean, I keep trying to think of something so I don’t sound dramatic. But the things that used to be “mine” don’t feel like anything now. Painting, hiking, choir, even Sunday family dinners—I used to make time for all of it. I’d be the one fussing over who was bringing dessert, which is silly, but it mattered to me.\n\nNow if I have a free hour, I just sort of sit there. I’ll look at my paints or think, “Maybe I should call someone,” and then I just… don’t. I can’t seem to care about it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about death, or wishing you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I haven’t really wanted to say that out loud, because it sounds so awful. But sometimes, late at night when I’m in the studio—well, it’s barely a studio, it’s just the spare room with my old paints and all the school supplies piled everywhere—I’ll catch myself thinking that it would be kind of a relief if I just didn’t wake up.\n\nI haven’t made a plan or anything like that. But the thoughts scare me. They scared me enough that I finally told my husband I couldn’t keep trying to handle it by myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge most of the time?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly. Maybe on edge sometimes, but it’s hard for me to tell what’s what anymore. I keep thinking of “anxious” like before a parent-teacher conference or when the copier jams five minutes before class—sorry, that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nMost of the time I just feel… not right. But I’m not sure I’ve noticed it as anxiety specifically.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t really been keeping track of it. My clothes maybe fit differently sometimes, but then I think, well, some of them are old anyway, and I’ve had the same work pants forever. I don’t weigh myself much… the scale is in the bathroom closet behind all the towels, and I keep meaning to clean that out.\n\nSo… I don’t know if it’s been a significant amount. I haven’t noticed something obvious like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving more slowly than you used to or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I don’t think I’ve noticed that exactly. Some mornings I move around like I’m underwater, but then I’ll also get distracted and start rearranging papers or sharpening pencils for no real reason—though that might just be because our house is always kind of cluttered.\n\nTrouble sitting still… maybe sometimes? But not in a way I’ve really thought about. I guess I’m having a hard time knowing what counts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving slower than you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I have moments where I feel like I’m taking longer to get going, like putting my shoes on or getting out the door, but then I think maybe everyone feels like that some days.\n\nAt school I’ll notice the kids are already lined up and I’m still gathering the attendance sheets, and then I start thinking about whether I left my mug in the staff room, so… I don’t know if that’s actually moving slower or just me being scattered. I haven’t really noticed it in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like it's harder to initiate activities or get started with things than it used to be?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s less like I can’t physically start, and more like I just… can’t make myself care enough to begin.\n\nI used to have things I’d look forward to—painting, going on hikes, choir practice, Sunday family get-togethers. I used to squeeze those in even when school was busy, you know, like I’d pack my bag the night before or leave my paints out on the table. Now if I have a free hour, I’ll just sit there. I’ll think, “I could paint,” or “I should call about choir,” and then I just don’t.\n\nThat’s kind of what finally made me come in, honestly. I don’t feel like myself anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really been tracking it. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different, I don’t think… though some days I avoid anything with a waistband anyway, so that’s maybe not the best measure.\n\nWe do have a scale somewhere, but it’s in the bathroom closet behind all these old towels and cleaning stuff—I keep meaning to clear that out. So… no, I haven’t noticed any big weight change. At least not that I could say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or restless when you're sitting or standing still?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way I’ve really noticed. I mean, I’ll shift around in a chair if it’s uncomfortable—those little plastic classroom chairs are awful, honestly, I don’t know how the kids sit in them all day—and sometimes I tap a pen if I’m holding one.\n\nBut restless like I can’t sit still? I don’t know. I wouldn’t say that’s been a big thing. I’m probably more just… sitting there and losing track of time than fidgeting.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Probably more than I should.\n\nIt’s mostly with this curriculum project at school. If something goes wrong—even something small, like a lesson not landing right or materials not being ready—I start thinking I’m letting the students down. And then it turns into this whole… spiral, I guess. Like my parents worked so hard so I could go to college and have opportunities, and here I am messing up something I’m supposed to be good at.\n\nI know, logically, curriculum stuff always has hiccups. Teachers adjust things all the time. But I still end up feeling like it’s my fault, like I’m wasting what people did for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.     "content": "Return the JSON now."

I’d be the one fussing over who was bringing dessert, which is silly, but it mattered to me.\n\nNow if I have a free hour, I just sort of sit there. I’ll look at my paints or think, “Maybe I should call someone,” and then I just… don’t. I can’t seem to care about it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about death, or wishing you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I haven’t really wanted to say that out loud, because it sounds so awful. But sometimes, late at night when I’m in the studio—well, it’s barely a studio, it’s just the spare room with my old paints and all the school supplies piled everywhere—I’ll catch myself thinking that it would be kind of a relief if I just didn’t wake up.\n\nI haven’t made a plan or anything like that. But the thoughts scare me. They scared me enough that I finally told my husband I couldn’t keep trying to handle it by myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge most of the time?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly. Maybe on edge sometimes, but it’s hard for me to tell what’s what anymore. I keep thinking of “anxious” like before a parent-teacher conference or when the copier jams five minutes before class—sorry, that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nMost of the time I just feel… not right. But I’m not sure I’ve noticed it as anxiety specifically.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t really been keeping track of it. My clothes maybe fit differently sometimes, but then I think, well, some of them are old anyway, and I’ve had the same work pants forever. I don’t weigh myself much… the scale is in the bathroom closet behind all the towels, and I keep meaning to clean that out.\n\nSo… I don’t know if it’s been a significant amount. I haven’t noticed something obvious like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving more slowly than you used to or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I don’t think I’ve noticed that exactly. Some mornings I move around like I’m underwater, but then I’ll also get distracted and start rearranging papers or sharpening pencils for no real reason—though that might just be because our house is always kind of cluttered.\n\nTrouble sitting still… maybe sometimes? But not in a way I’ve really thought about. I guess I’m having a hard time knowing what counts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving slower than you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I have moments where I feel like I’m taking longer to get going, like putting my shoes on or getting out the door, but then I think maybe everyone feels like that some days.\n\nAt school I’ll notice the kids are already lined up and I’m still gathering the attendance sheets, and then I start thinking about whether I left my mug in the staff room, so… I don’t know if that’s actually moving slower or just me being scattered. I haven’t really noticed it in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like it's harder to initiate activities or get started with things than it used to be?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s less like I can’t physically start, and more like I just… can’t make myself care enough to begin.\n\nI used to have things I’d look forward to—painting, going on hikes, choir practice, Sunday family get-togethers. I used to squeeze those in even when school was busy, you know, like I’d pack my bag the night before or leave my paints out on the table. Now if I have a free hour, I’ll just sit there. I’ll think, “I could paint,” or “I should call about choir,” and then I just don’t.\n\nThat’s kind of what finally made me come in, honestly. I don’t feel like myself anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really been tracking it. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different, I don’t think… though some days I avoid anything with a waistband anyway, so that’s maybe not the best measure.\n\nWe do have a scale somewhere, but it’s in the bathroom closet behind all these old towels and cleaning stuff—I keep meaning to clear that out. So… no, I haven’t noticed any big weight change. At least not that I could say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or restless when you're sitting or standing still?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way I’ve really noticed. I mean, I’ll shift around in a chair if it’s uncomfortable—those little plastic classroom chairs are awful, honestly, I don’t know how the kids sit in them all day—and sometimes I tap a pen if I’m holding one.\n\nBut restless like I can’t sit still? I don’t know. I wouldn’t say that’s been a big thing. I’m probably more just… sitting there and losing track of time than fidgeting.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Probably more than I should.\n\nIt’s mostly with this curriculum project at school. If something goes wrong—even something small, like a lesson not landing right or materials not being ready—I start thinking I’m letting the students down. And then it turns into this whole… spiral, I guess. Like my parents worked so hard so I could go to college and have opportunities, and here I am messing up something I’m supposed to be good at.\n\nI know, logically, curriculum stuff always has hiccups. Teachers adjust things all the time. But I still end up feeling like it’s my fault, like I’m wasting what people did for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing things like pacing back and forth or feeling like you need to be in constant motion?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like pacing, no. I mean, if I’m waiting for the kettle or something I might wander around the kitchen, but that’s more because there’s always dishes or mail sitting there, and then I notice the plant needs water—actually I’ve been terrible about that lately, the one by the window is looking sad.\n\nBut constant motion? No, I wouldn’t say that’s me. If anything, I can just sit there longer than I mean to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I don’t pick up the brushes or text anyone. It kind of started building when I took over this STEM-Arts curriculum initiative at the school. At first I told myself I was just tired or stretched thin—because, you know, teachers always have some new project with a catchy name—but it didn’t really let up. It just kept getting worse from there.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Not really… or, I mean, I keep trying to think of something so I don’t sound dramatic. But the things that used to be “mine” don’t feel like anything now. Painting, hiking, choir, even Sunday family dinners—I used to make time for all of it. I’d be the one fussing over who was bringing dessert, which is silly, but it mattered to me.\n\nNow if I have a free hour, I just sort of sit there. I’ll look at my paints or think, “Maybe I should call someone,” and then I just… don’t. I can’t seem to care about it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about death, or wishing you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I haven’t really wanted to say that out loud, because it sounds so awful. But sometimes, late at night when I’m in the studio—well, it’s barely a studio, it’s just the spare room with my old paints and all the school supplies piled everywhere—I’ll catch myself thinking that it would be kind of a relief if I just didn’t wake up.\n\nI haven’t made a plan or anything like that. But the thoughts scare me. They scared me enough that I finally told my husband I couldn’t keep trying to handle it by myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge most of the time?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly. Maybe on edge sometimes, but it’s hard for me to tell what’s what anymore. I keep thinking of “anxious” like before a parent-teacher conference or when the copier jams five minutes before class—sorry, that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nMost of the time I just feel… not right. But I’m not sure I’ve noticed it as anxiety specifically.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t really been keeping track of it. My clothes maybe fit differently sometimes, but then I think, well, some of them are old anyway, and I’ve had the same work pants forever. I don’t weigh myself much… the scale is in the bathroom closet behind all the towels, and I keep meaning to clean that out.\n\nSo… I don’t know if it’s been a significant amount. I haven’t noticed something obvious like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving more slowly than you used to or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I don’t think I’ve noticed that exactly. Some mornings I move around like I’m underwater, but then I’ll also get distracted and start rearranging papers or sharpening pencils for no real reason—though that might just be because our house is always kind of cluttered.\n\nTrouble sitting still… maybe sometimes? But not in a way I’ve really thought about. I guess I’m having a hard time knowing what counts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving slower than you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I have moments where I feel like I’m taking longer to get going, like putting my shoes on or getting out the door, but then I think maybe everyone feels like that some days.\n\nAt school I’ll notice the kids are already lined up and I’m still gathering the attendance sheets, and then I start thinking about whether I left my mug in the staff room, so… I don’t know if that’s actually moving slower or just me being scattered. I haven’t really noticed it in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like it's harder to initiate activities or get started with things than it used to be?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s less like I can’t physically start, and more like I just… can’t make myself care enough to begin.\n\nI used to have things I’d look forward to—painting, going on hikes, choir practice, Sunday family get-togethers. I used to squeeze those in even when school was busy, you know, like I’d pack my bag the night before or leave my paints out on the table. Now if I have a free hour, I’ll just sit there. I’ll think, “I could paint,” or “I should call about choir,” and then I just don’t.\n\nThat’s kind of what finally made me come in, honestly. I don’t feel like myself anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really been tracking it. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different, I don’t think… though some days I avoid anything with a waistband anyway, so that’s maybe not the best measure.\n\nWe do have a scale somewhere, but it’s in the bathroom closet behind all these old towels and cleaning stuff—I keep meaning to clear that out. So… no, I haven’t noticed any big weight change. At least not that I could say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or restless when you're sitting or standing still?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way I’ve really noticed. I mean, I’ll shift around in a chair if it’s uncomfortable—those little plastic classroom chairs are awful, honestly, I don’t know how the kids sit in them all day—and sometimes I tap a pen if I’m holding one.\n\nBut restless like I can’t sit still? I don’t know. I wouldn’t say that’s been a big thing. I’m probably more just… sitting there and losing track of time than fidgeting.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Probably more than I should.\n\nIt’s mostly with this curriculum project at school. If something goes wrong—even something small, like a lesson not landing right or materials not being ready—I start thinking I’m letting the students down. And then it turns into this whole… spiral, I guess. Like my parents worked so hard so I could go to college and have opportunities, and here I am messing up something I’m supposed to be good at.\n\nI know, logically, curriculum stuff always has hiccups. Teachers adjust things all the time. But I still end up feeling like it’s my fault, like I’m wasting what people did for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing things like pacing back and forth or feeling like you need to be in constant motion?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like pacing, no. I mean, if I’m waiting for the kettle or something I might wander around the kitchen, but that’s more because there’s always dishes or mail sitting there, and then I notice the plant needs water—actually I’ve been terrible about that lately, the one by the window is looking sad.\n\nBut constant motion? No, I wouldn’t say that’s me. If anything, I can just sit there longer than I mean to.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost more than 5% of your body weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly, so I don’t know what the number would be.\n\nMy clothes maybe fit a little differently sometimes, but that could just be… laundry shrinking things, or me wearing the same cardigan too much. I have this one black cardigan I basically live in at school because the classrooms are freezing, and I’m always meaning to get another one but then I forget.\n\nSo… I don’t think I can say I’ve lost that much. I just haven’t really tracked it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I have. Not like—I haven’t sat down and made some plan or anything like that.

It’s more, on those really late nights in the studio, when it’s quiet and I’m still trying to fix something for the curriculum… I’ll suddenly think, “It would be such a relief if I just didn’t wake up.” And then I feel awful for even thinking it. The studio gets so cold at night too, which is silly to mention, but I notice things like the hum of the lights and the mess of papers everywhere, and my mind just goes there.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I just don’t feel like myself anymore. I used to always have things I cared about—painting, hiking, choir, Sunday family get-togethers. I mean, my sister used to tease me because I’d be the one organizing who brought dessert, and now even if I have a free hour, I just sort of sit there. I don’t pick up the brushes, I don’t call anyone, I don’t even really care about going. That’s what finally made me think I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy the things you used to, or has the pleasure gone away?\n\nPatient: The pleasure’s mostly gone, yeah. I mean, I used to really make time for things—painting especially, and hiking, choir, the Sunday family stuff. My sister used to joke I treated those dinners like some kind of military operation with desserts and who was bringing what… and now, even if I’ve got a free hour, I just sit there. I don’t pick up the brushes or text anyone. It’s like I know those things used to matter to me, but I can’t really feel why.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think… about eight months now? It kind of started building when I took over this STEM-Arts curriculum initiative at the school. At first I told myself I was just tired or stretched thin—because, you know, teachers always have some new project with a catchy name—but it didn’t really let up. It just kept getting worse from there.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Not really… or, I mean, I keep trying to think of something so I don’t sound dramatic. But the things that used to be “mine” don’t feel like anything now. Painting, hiking, choir, even Sunday family dinners—I used to make time for all of it. I’d be the one fussing over who was bringing dessert, which is silly, but it mattered to me.\n\nNow if I have a free hour, I just sort of sit there. I’ll look at my paints or think, “Maybe I should call someone,” and then I just… don’t. I can’t seem to care about it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about death, or wishing you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I haven’t really wanted to say that out loud, because it sounds so awful. But sometimes, late at night when I’m in the studio—well, it’s barely a studio, it’s just the spare room with my old paints and all the school supplies piled everywhere—I’ll catch myself thinking that it would be kind of a relief if I just didn’t wake up.\n\nI haven’t made a plan or anything like that. But the thoughts scare me. They scared me enough that I finally told my husband I couldn’t keep trying to handle it by myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge most of the time?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly. Maybe on edge sometimes, but it’s hard for me to tell what’s what anymore. I keep thinking of “anxious” like before a parent-teacher conference or when the copier jams five minutes before class—sorry, that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nMost of the time I just feel… not right. But I’m not sure I’ve noticed it as anxiety specifically.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t really been keeping track of it. My clothes maybe fit differently sometimes, but then I think, well, some of them are old anyway, and I’ve had the same work pants forever. I don’t weigh myself much… the scale is in the bathroom closet behind all the towels, and I keep meaning to clean that out.\n\nSo… I don’t know if it’s been a significant amount. I haven’t noticed something obvious like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving more slowly than you used to or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I don’t think I’ve noticed that exactly. Some mornings I move around like I’m underwater, but then I’ll also get distracted and start rearranging papers or sharpening pencils for no real reason—though that might just be because our house is always kind of cluttered.\n\nTrouble sitting still… maybe sometimes? But not in a way I’ve really thought about. I guess I’m having a hard time knowing what counts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving slower than you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I have moments where I feel like I’m taking longer to get going, like putting my shoes on or getting out the door, but then I think maybe everyone feels like that some days.\n\nAt school I’ll notice the kids are already lined up and I’m still gathering the attendance sheets, and then I start thinking about whether I left my mug in the staff room, so… I don’t know if that’s actually moving slower or just me being scattered. I haven’t really noticed it in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like it's harder to initiate activities or get started with things than it used to be?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s less like I can’t physically start, and more like I just… can’t make myself care enough to begin.\n\nI used to have things I’d look forward to—painting, going on hikes, choir practice, Sunday family get-togethers. I used to squeeze those in even when school was busy, you know, like I’d pack my bag the night before or leave my paints out on the table. Now if I have a free hour, I’ll just sit there. I’ll think, “I could paint,” or “I should call about choir,” and then I just don’t.\n\nThat’s kind of what finally made me come in, honestly. I don’t feel like myself anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really been tracking it. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different, I don’t think… though some days I avoid anything with a waistband anyway, so that’s maybe not the best measure.\n\nWe do have a scale somewhere, but it’s in the bathroom closet behind all these old towels and cleaning stuff—I keep meaning to clear that out. So… no, I haven’t noticed any big weight change. At least not that I could say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or restless when you're sitting or standing still?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way I’ve really noticed. I mean, I’ll shift around in a chair if it’s uncomfortable—those little plastic classroom chairs are awful, honestly, I don’t know how the kids sit in them all day—and sometimes I tap a pen if I’m holding one.\n\nBut restless like I can’t sit still? I don’t know. I wouldn’t say that’s been a big thing. I’m probably more just… sitting there and losing track of time than fidgeting.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Probably more than I should.\n\nIt’s mostly with this curriculum project at school. If something goes wrong—even something small, like a lesson not landing right or materials not being ready—I start thinking I’m letting the students down. And then it turns into this whole… spiral, I guess. Like my parents worked so hard so I could go to college and have opportunities, and here I am messing up something I’m supposed to be good at.\n\nI know, logically, curriculum stuff always has hiccups. Teachers adjust things all the time. But I still end up feeling like it’s my fault, like I’m wasting what people did for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing things like pacing back and forth or feeling like you need to be in constant motion?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like pacing, no. I mean, if I’m waiting for the kettle or something I might wander around the kitchen, but that’s more because there’s always dishes or mail sitting there, and then I notice the plant needs water—actually I’ve been terrible about that lately, the one by the window is looking sad.\n\nBut constant motion? No, I wouldn’t say that’s me. If anything, I can just sit there longer than I mean to.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost more than 5% of your body weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly, so I don’t know what the number would be.\n\nMy clothes maybe fit a little differently sometimes, but that could just be… laundry shrinking things, or me wearing the same cardigan too much. I have this one black cardigan I basically live in at school because the classrooms are freezing, and I’m always meaning to get another one but then I forget.\n\nSo… I don’t think I can say I’ve lost that much. I just haven’t really tracked it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having recurring thoughts about death or wanting to be dead?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Not like—I haven’t sat down and made some plan or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more, on those really late nights in the studio, when it’s quiet and I’m still trying to fix something for the curriculum… I’ll suddenly think, “It would be such a relief if I just didn’t wake up.” And then I feel awful for even thinking it. The studio gets so cold at night too, which is silly to mention, but I notice things like the hum of the lights and the mess of papers everywhere, and my mind just goes there.\n\nIt scared me enough that I told my husband I couldn’t keep handling those thoughts by myself.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

The studio gets so cold at night too, which is silly to mention, but I notice things like the hum of the lights and the mess of papers everywhere, and my mind just goes there.\n\nIt scared me enough that I told my husband I couldn’t keep handling those thoughts by myself.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself doing things like pulling your hair, biting your nails, or engaging in other repetitive behaviors when you're feeling anxious or restless?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / teaching_assistant / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… I mean, I might pick at a cuticle if I notice it, but I wouldn’t say I sit there pulling my hair or biting my nails.

Sometimes I’ll fiddle with things, like the edge of a paper or a pen cap, especially if I’m at my desk and there’s all that clutter around—student worksheets, sticky notes, those awful little paper clips that end up everywhere. But I don’t know if that counts as repetitive behavior or if it’s just… being fidgety sometimes.

So, no, not anything I’ve really noticed as a pattern.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I just don’t feel like myself anymore. I used to always have things I cared about—painting, hiking, choir, Sunday family get-togethers. I mean, my sister used to tease me because I’d be the one organizing who brought dessert, and now even if I have a free hour, I just sort of sit there. I don’t pick up the brushes, I don’t call anyone, I don’t even really care about going. That’s what finally made me think I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy the things you used to, or has the pleasure gone away?\n\nPatient: The pleasure’s mostly gone, yeah. I mean, I used to really make time for things—painting especially, and hiking, choir, the Sunday family stuff. My sister used to joke I treated those dinners like some kind of military operation with desserts and who was bringing what… and now, even if I’ve got a free hour, I just sit there. I don’t pick up the brushes or text anyone. It’s like I know those things used to matter to me, but I can’t really feel why.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think… about eight months now? It kind of started building when I took over this STEM-Arts curriculum initiative at the school. At first I told myself I was just tired or stretched thin—because, you know, teachers always have some new project with a catchy name—but it didn’t really let up. It just kept getting worse from there.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Not really… or, I mean, I keep trying to think of something so I don’t sound dramatic. But the things that used to be “mine” don’t feel like anything now. Painting, hiking, choir, even Sunday family dinners—I used to make time for all of it. I’d be the one fussing over who was bringing dessert, which is silly, but it mattered to me.\n\nNow if I have a free hour, I just sort of sit there. I’ll look at my paints or think, “Maybe I should call someone,” and then I just… don’t. I can’t seem to care about it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about death, or wishing you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I haven’t really wanted to say that out loud, because it sounds so awful. But sometimes, late at night when I’m in the studio—well, it’s barely a studio, it’s just the spare room with my old paints and all the school supplies piled everywhere—I’ll catch myself thinking that it would be kind of a relief if I just didn’t wake up.\n\nI haven’t made a plan or anything like that. But the thoughts scare me. They scared me enough that I finally told my husband I couldn’t keep trying to handle it by myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge most of the time?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly. Maybe on edge sometimes, but it’s hard for me to tell what’s what anymore. I keep thinking of “anxious” like before a parent-teacher conference or when the copier jams five minutes before class—sorry, that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nMost of the time I just feel… not right. But I’m not sure I’ve noticed it as anxiety specifically.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t really been keeping track of it. My clothes maybe fit differently sometimes, but then I think, well, some of them are old anyway, and I’ve had the same work pants forever. I don’t weigh myself much… the scale is in the bathroom closet behind all the towels, and I keep meaning to clean that out.\n\nSo… I don’t know if it’s been a significant amount. I haven’t noticed something obvious like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving more slowly than you used to or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I don’t think I’ve noticed that exactly. Some mornings I move around like I’m underwater, but then I’ll also get distracted and start rearranging papers or sharpening pencils for no real reason—though that might just be because our house is always kind of cluttered.\n\nTrouble sitting still… maybe sometimes? But not in a way I’ve really thought about. I guess I’m having a hard time knowing what counts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving slower than you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I have moments where I feel like I’m taking longer to get going, like putting my shoes on or getting out the door, but then I think maybe everyone feels like that some days.\n\nAt school I’ll notice the kids are already lined up and I’m still gathering the attendance sheets, and then I start thinking about whether I left my mug in the staff room, so… I don’t know if that’s actually moving slower or just me being scattered. I haven’t really noticed it in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like it's harder to initiate activities or get started with things than it used to be?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s less like I can’t physically start, and more like I just… can’t make myself care enough to begin.\n\nI used to have things I’d look forward to—painting, going on hikes, choir practice, Sunday family get-togethers. I used to squeeze those in even when school was busy, you know, like I’d pack my bag the night before or leave my paints out on the table. Now if I have a free hour, I’ll just sit there. I’ll think, “I could paint,” or “I should call about choir,” and then I just don’t.\n\nThat’s kind of what finally made me come in, honestly. I don’t feel like myself anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really been tracking it. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different, I don’t think… though some days I avoid anything with a waistband anyway, so that’s maybe not the best measure.\n\nWe do have a scale somewhere, but it’s in the bathroom closet behind all these old towels and cleaning stuff—I keep meaning to clear that out. So… no, I haven’t noticed any big weight change. At least not that I could say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or restless when you're sitting or standing still?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way I’ve really noticed. I mean, I’ll shift around in a chair if it’s uncomfortable—those little plastic classroom chairs are awful, honestly, I don’t know how the kids sit in them all day—and sometimes I tap a pen if I’m holding one.\n\nBut restless like I can’t sit still? I don’t know. I wouldn’t say that’s been a big thing. I’m probably more just… sitting there and losing track of time than fidgeting.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Probably more than I should.\n\nIt’s mostly with this curriculum project at school. If something goes wrong—even something small, like a lesson not landing right or materials not being ready—I start thinking I’m letting the students down. And then it turns into this whole… spiral, I guess. Like my parents worked so hard so I could go to college and have opportunities, and here I am messing up something I’m supposed to be good at.\n\nI know, logically, curriculum stuff always has hiccups. Teachers adjust things all the time. But I still end up feeling like it’s my fault, like I’m wasting what people did for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing things like pacing back and forth or feeling like you need to be in constant motion?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like pacing, no. I mean, if I’m waiting for the kettle or something I might wander around the kitchen, but that’s more because there’s always dishes or mail sitting there, and then I notice the plant needs water—actually I’ve been terrible about that lately, the one by the window is looking sad.\n\nBut constant motion? No, I wouldn’t say that’s me. If anything, I can just sit there longer than I mean to.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost more than 5% of your body weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly, so I don’t know what the number would be.\n\nMy clothes maybe fit a little differently sometimes, but that could just be… laundry shrinking things, or me wearing the same cardigan too much. I have this one black cardigan I basically live in at school because the classrooms are freezing, and I’m always meaning to get another one but then I forget.\n\nSo… I don’t think I can say I’ve lost that much. I just haven’t really tracked it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having recurring thoughts about death or wanting to be dead?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I just haven’t really tracked it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having recurring thoughts about death or wanting to be dead?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Not like—I haven’t sat down and made some plan or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more, on those really late nights in the studio, when it’s quiet and I’m still trying to fix something for the curriculum… I’ll suddenly think, “It would be such a relief if I just didn’t wake up.” And then I feel awful for even thinking it. The studio gets so cold at night too, which is silly to mention, but I notice things like the hum of the lights and the mess of papers everywhere, and my mind just goes there.\n\nIt scared me enough that I told my husband I couldn’t keep handling those thoughts by myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing things like pulling your hair, biting your nails, or engaging in other repetitive behaviors when you're feeling anxious or restless?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I might pick at a cuticle if I notice it, but I wouldn’t say I sit there pulling my hair or biting my nails.\n\nSometimes I’ll fiddle with things, like the edge of a paper or a pen cap, especially if I’m at my desk and there’s all that clutter around—student worksheets, sticky notes, those awful little paper clips that end up everywhere. But I don’t know if that counts as repetitive behavior or if it’s just… being fidgety sometimes.\n\nSo, no, not anything I’ve really noticed as a pattern.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often find yourself doing things like pulling your hair, biting your nails, or engaging in other repetitive behaviors when you're sitting still?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… not often, anyway. If I’m sitting still I might mess with whatever’s in front of me, like a pen or the corner of a paper, especially if I’m waiting for someone to finish talking or I’m in one of those staff meetings that goes on forever.

But pulling my hair or biting my nails, no, I haven’t really noticed that. I mean, I do sometimes realize I’ve been holding a paper clip and bending it back and forth, and then I feel ridiculous because then it’s useless and I have to throw it away. We have this one drawer at school that’s just full of bent paper clips and dried-out markers, which drives me crazy, but nobody ever cleans it out.

So… I guess I fidget a little, but not anything like a real habit that I’m aware of.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I just don’t feel like myself anymore. I used to always have things I cared about—painting, hiking, choir, Sunday family get-togethers. I mean, my sister used to tease me because I’d be the one organizing who brought dessert, and now even if I have a free hour, I just sort of sit there. I don’t pick up the brushes, I don’t call anyone, I don’t even really care about going. That’s what finally made me think I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy the things you used to, or has the pleasure gone away?\n\nPatient: The pleasure’s mostly gone, yeah. I mean, I used to really make time for things—painting especially, and hiking, choir, the Sunday family stuff. My sister used to joke I treated those dinners like some kind of military operation with desserts and who was bringing what… and now, even if I’ve got a free hour, I just sit there. I don’t pick up the brushes or text anyone. It’s like I know those things used to matter to me, but I can’t really feel why.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think… about eight months now? It kind of started building when I took over this STEM-Arts curriculum initiative at the school. At first I told myself I was just tired or stretched thin—because, you know, teachers always have some new project with a catchy name—but it didn’t really let up. It just kept getting worse from there.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Not really… or, I mean, I keep trying to think of something so I don’t sound dramatic. But the things that used to be “mine” don’t feel like anything now. Painting, hiking, choir, even Sunday family dinners—I used to make time for all of it. I’d be the one fussing over who was bringing dessert, which is silly, but it mattered to me.\n\nNow if I have a free hour, I just sort of sit there. I’ll look at my paints or think, “Maybe I should call someone,” and then I just… don’t. I can’t seem to care about it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about death, or wishing you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I haven’t really wanted to say that out loud, because it sounds so awful. But sometimes, late at night when I’m in the studio—well, it’s barely a studio, it’s just the spare room with my old paints and all the school supplies piled everywhere—I’ll catch myself thinking that it would be kind of a relief if I just didn’t wake up.\n\nI haven’t made a plan or anything like that. But the thoughts scare me. They scared me enough that I finally told my husband I couldn’t keep trying to handle it by myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge most of the time?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly. Maybe on edge sometimes, but it’s hard for me to tell what’s what anymore. I keep thinking of “anxious” like before a parent-teacher conference or when the copier jams five minutes before class—sorry, that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nMost of the time I just feel… not right. But I’m not sure I’ve noticed it as anxiety specifically.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t really been keeping track of it. My clothes maybe fit differently sometimes, but then I think, well, some of them are old anyway, and I’ve had the same work pants forever. I don’t weigh myself much… the scale is in the bathroom closet behind all the towels, and I keep meaning to clean that out.\n\nSo… I don’t know if it’s been a significant amount. I haven’t noticed something obvious like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving more slowly than you used to or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I don’t think I’ve noticed that exactly. Some mornings I move around like I’m underwater, but then I’ll also get distracted and start rearranging papers or sharpening pencils for no real reason—though that might just be because our house is always kind of cluttered.\n\nTrouble sitting still… maybe sometimes? But not in a way I’ve really thought about. I guess I’m having a hard time knowing what counts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving slower than you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I have moments where I feel like I’m taking longer to get going, like putting my shoes on or getting out the door, but then I think maybe everyone feels like that some days.\n\nAt school I’ll notice the kids are already lined up and I’m still gathering the attendance sheets, and then I start thinking about whether I left my mug in the staff room, so… I don’t know if that’s actually moving slower or just me being scattered. I haven’t really noticed it in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like it's harder to initiate activities or get started with things than it used to be?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s less like I can’t physically start, and more like I just… can’t make myself care enough to begin.\n\nI used to have things I’d look forward to—painting, going on hikes, choir practice, Sunday family get-togethers. I used to squeeze those in even when school was busy, you know, like I’d pack my bag the night before or leave my paints out on the table. Now if I have a free hour, I’ll just sit there. I’ll think, “I could paint,” or “I should call about choir,” and then I just don’t.\n\nThat’s kind of what finally made me come in, honestly. I don’t feel like myself anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really been tracking it. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different, I don’t think… though some days I avoid anything with a waistband anyway, so that’s maybe not the best measure.\n\nWe do have a scale somewhere, but it’s in the bathroom closet behind all these old towels and cleaning stuff—I keep meaning to clear that out. So… no, I haven’t noticed any big weight change. At least not that I could say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or restless when you're sitting or standing still?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way I’ve really noticed. I mean, I’ll shift around in a chair if it’s uncomfortable—those little plastic classroom chairs are awful, honestly, I don’t know how the kids sit in them all day—and sometimes I tap a pen if I’m holding one.\n\nBut restless like I can’t sit still? I don’t know. I wouldn’t say that’s been a big thing. I’m probably more just… sitting there and losing track of time than fidgeting.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Probably more than I should.\n\nIt’s mostly with this curriculum project at school. If something goes wrong—even something small, like a lesson not landing right or materials not being ready—I start thinking I’m letting the students down. And then it turns into this whole… spiral, I guess. Like my parents worked so hard so I could go to college and have opportunities, and here I am messing up something I’m supposed to be good at.\n\nI know, logically, curriculum stuff always has hiccups. Teachers adjust things all the time. But I still end up feeling like it’s my fault, like I’m wasting what people did for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing things like pacing back and forth or feeling like you need to be in constant motion?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like pacing, no. I mean, if I’m waiting for the kettle or something I might wander around the kitchen, but that’s more because there’s always dishes or mail sitting there, and then I notice the plant needs water—actually I’ve been terrible about that lately, the one by the window is looking sad.\n\nBut constant motion? No, I wouldn’t say that’s me. If anything, I can just sit there longer than I mean to.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost more than 5% of your body weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly, so I don’t know what the number would be.\n\nMy clothes maybe fit a little differently sometimes, but that could just be… laundry shrinking things, or me wearing the same cardigan too much. I have this one black cardigan I basically live in at school because the classrooms are freezing, and I’m always meaning to get another one but then I forget.\n\nSo… I don’t think I can say I’ve lost that much. I mean, my sister used to tease me because I’d be the one organizing who brought dessert, and now even if I have a free hour, I just sort of sit there. I don’t pick up the brushes, I don’t call anyone, I don’t even really care about going. That’s what finally made me think I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy the things you used to, or has the pleasure gone away?\n\nPatient: The pleasure’s mostly gone, yeah. I mean, I used to really make time for things—painting especially, and hiking, choir, the Sunday family stuff. My sister used to joke I treated those dinners like some kind of military operation with desserts and who was bringing what… and now, even if I’ve got a free hour, I just sit there. I don’t pick up the brushes or text anyone. It’s like I know those things used to matter to me, but I can’t really feel why.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think… about eight months now? I can’t seem to care about it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about death, or wishing you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I haven’t really wanted to say that out loud, because it sounds so awful. But I’m not sure I’ve noticed it as anxiety specifically.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t really been keeping track of it. My clothes maybe fit differently sometimes, but then I think, well, some of them are old anyway, and I’ve had the same work pants forever. I don’t weigh myself much… the scale is in the bathroom closet behind all the towels, and I keep meaning to clean that out.\n\nSo… I don’t know if it’s been a significant amount. I haven’t noticed something obvious like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving more slowly than you used to or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I don’t think I’ve noticed that exactly. But not in a way I’ve really thought about. I guess I’m having a hard time knowing what counts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving slower than you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I have moments where I feel like I’m taking longer to get going, like putting my shoes on or getting out the door, but then I think maybe everyone feels like that some days.\n\nAt school I’ll notice the kids are already lined up and I’m still gathering the attendance sheets, and then I start thinking about whether I left my mug in the staff room, so… I don’t know if that’s actually moving slower or just me being scattered. It’s less like I can’t physically start, and more like I just… can’t make myself care enough to begin.\n\nI used to have things I’d look forward to—painting, going on hikes, choir practice, Sunday family get-togethers. I used to squeeze those in even when school was busy, you know, like I’d pack my bag the night before or leave my paints out on the table. Now if I have a free hour, I’ll just sit there. I’ll think, “I could paint,” or “I should call about choir,” and then I just don’t.\n\nThat’s kind of what finally made me come in, honestly. I don’t feel like myself anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I’ll shift around in a chair if it’s uncomfortable—those little plastic classroom chairs are awful, honestly, I don’t know how the kids sit in them all day—and sometimes I tap a pen if I’m holding one.\n\nBut restless like I can’t sit still? I don’t know. I wouldn’t say that’s been a big thing. No, I wouldn’t say that’s me. If anything, I can just sit there longer than I mean to.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost more than 5% of your body weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly, so I don’t know what the number would be.\n\nMy clothes maybe fit a little differently sometimes, but that could just be… laundry shrinking things, or me wearing the same cardigan too much.
